    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data---not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nThere is no per-passage lineage across depths, ensuring cross-validation splits do not introduce information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using a fixed reference model---Pythia-1.4B---providing a consistent perplexity reference across all generation conditions.\nWe select Pythia-1.4B because it is the smallest of the three generators, and its training corpus (The Pile) partially overlaps with those of OLMo and GPT-2~XL, keeping perplexity values in a comparable range.\nFor the Pythia pipeline, feature extraction constitutes self-model measurement; for OLMo and GPT-2~XL, it is cross-model.\nEmpirically, the Pythia pipeline's 4-class accuracy (78.2\\%) does not exceed GPT-2~XL (85.4\\%), indicating that self-model measurement does not confer a systematic advantage.\nIn cross-model leave-one-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), the reference model remains fixed regardless of which generator is held out; the implications of sharing the reference model with one generator are discussed in Limitations.\nAn ablation replacing Pythia-1.4B with OLMo-1B as reference model yields 74.5\\% vs 78.5\\%, suggesting that depth signal detection is not critically dependent on the specific reference model ($\\Delta$=4.0pp, within the 5pp robustness threshold; Appendix~\\ref{tab:ref_ablation}).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features, placing the effective independent dimensionality at ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) to formalize the frontier beyond which adjacent depths become statistically indistinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\;     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nWe formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ across models; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.\nRecent work corroborates this connection, identifying entropy decline as a measurable signal of recursive degradation that parallels the tail erosion our features capture~\\cite{shi2025_closer_look}.\nZero-shot LLM baselines reinforce this view: DeepSeek V3.2 (36.2\\%) and Qwen2.5-7B (30.4\\%) exhibit complementary failure modes---the former predicts nearly all samples as d0 while the latter is biased toward d1---yet both perform near chance, confirming that depth discrimination requires the targeted statistical features our pipeline extracts rather than general language understanding.\n\nCross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.\nA multi-temperature ablation ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 1.1]$, Pythia-1.4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}) reinforces this: within-$\\tau$ depth discrimination is robust for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$), but cross-$\\tau$ transfer (mean 46.7\\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%), confirming that strategy-conditioned classifiers are necessary.\nA two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this.\nDifferent strategies produce distinguishable perplexity signatures (top-$k$ yields narrower distributions; temperature sampling produces heavier tails), suggesting that automatic strategy identification is technically feasible, though independent validation on held-out strategy sets is needed.\nAs an alternative, when deployment is restricted to a single platform or API provider, the decoding strategy is typically known a priori, and a strategy-conditioned classifier can be applied directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nDepth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) while filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nA proof-of-concept LoRA fine-tuning experiment shows RF depth-based filtering matches unfiltered performance with 44\\% of the data, significantly outperforming size-matched random subsampling at all stringency tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}).\nCross-strategy deployment would first require strategy identification to avoid boundary inversion (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods---watermarking~\\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} (requires generator cooperation) and source attribution~\\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} (identifies model, not history)---by characterizing \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator identity~\\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}.\nA layered system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}\nSliding-window depth estimation at the segment level (256--512 tokens) could extend the approach to mixed-depth documents, enabling paragraph-level curation even from partially contaminated content.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nPerplexity tail statistics exhibit inherent resistance to surface-level transformations (paraphrasing, back-translation) that preserve surprisal structure, though robustness to targeted distribution-aware attacks remains unverified.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling behavior.}\nOur cross-scale experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) confirm that depth signal persists at 7--8B scale (Llama-3.1-8B: 70.2\\%, Qwen2.5-7B: 60.1\\%).\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ with monotonic AUC decay, while Qwen2.5-7B achieves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$ via a non-monotonic (U-shaped) trajectory---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ at 1--1.5B.\nPer-step signal strength decreases (mean consecutive AUC ${\\approx}$0.78 vs.\\ ${\\approx}$0.85 at 1--1.5B), consistent with larger models generating more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories (monotonic for Llama-3.1 vs.\\ U-shaped for Qwen2.5-Instruct) suggest that instruction tuning may alter degradation dynamics at high recursion depth, an observation warranting further investigation with controlled base/instruct pairs.\nTail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution rather than model capacity per se, explaining why the signal attenuates but does not vanish; further scaling beyond 8B may require richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) to maintain discriminability.\n",

          " The dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.",

directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nDepth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) while filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nA pilot study (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive comparison between RF-filtered and unfiltered conditions ($p>0.4$, $\\mathrm{df}{=}4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation is needed to determine whether depth-based filtering provides a practical advantage over unfiltered training.\nCross-strategy deployment would first require methods---watermarking~\\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} (requires generator cooperation) and source attribution~\\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} (identifies model, not history)---by characterizing \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator identity~\\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}.\nA layered system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}\nSliding-window depth estimation at the segment level (256--512 tokens) could extend the approach to mixed-depth documents, enabling paragraph-level curation even from partially contaminated content.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nPerplexity tail statistics exhibit inherent resistance to surface-level transformations (paraphrasing, back-translation) that preserve surprisal structure, though robustness to targeted distribution-aware attacks remains unverified.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling behavior.}\nOur cross-scale experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) confirm that depth signal persists at 7--8B scale (Llama-3.1-8B: 70.2\\%, Qwen2.5-7B: 60.1\\%).\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ with monotonic AUC decay, while Qwen2.5-7B achieves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$ via a non-monotonic (U-shaped) trajectory---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ at 1--1.5B.\nPer-step signal strength decreases (mean consecutive AUC ${\\approx}$0.78 vs.\\ ${\\approx}$0.85 at 1--1.5B), consistent with larger models generating more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories (monotonic for Llama-3.1 vs.\\ U-shaped for Qwen2.5-Instruct) suggest that instruction tuning may alter degradation dynamics at high recursion depth, an observation warranting further investigation with controlled base/instruct pairs.\nTail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution rather than model capacity per se, explaining why the signal attenuates but does not vanish; further scaling beyond 8B may require richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) to maintain     "oldString": "The 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) in 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).",
    "newString": "Perplexity tail statistics as a feature group dominate depth discrimination: the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) in 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\\footnote{Individual feature rankings should be interpreted cautiously given high multicollinearity (VIF${>}$10 for 14--16/19 features); the group-level dominance of perplexity tail statistics is robust across importance measures.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 69.6\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\\% chance baseline by a wide margin.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) across the $3 \\times 3$ atlas, with d1--d3 three-class accuracy in parentheses (chance = 25\\% / 33\\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all cells.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2\\,(71.2) & 76.3\\,(70.6) & 69.6\\,(58.8) \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8\\,(75.0) & 82.0\\,(76.3) & 74.2\\,(66.1) \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3\\,(79.0) & 85.4\\,(82.6) & 76.6\\,(69.5) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-class recall distribution.}\nDepth-0 recall exceeds 98\\% in all 9 cells, confirming that human-written text is trivially separable from any generation depth.\nAmong generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (46--73\\% across cells), followed by d3 (58--82\\%) and d1 (72--91\\%).\nFor example, Pythia-1.4B nucleus achieves per-class recall of d0$\\,{=}\\,$99\\%, d1$\\,{=}\\,$82\\%, d2$\\,{=}\\,$59\\%, d3$\\,{=}\\,$72\\%.\nThe strongest d2 recall is obtained by GPT-2~XL nucleus (73\\%).\nThe d2 trough reflects its position at the center of the depth gradient: d2 features overlap with both the d1 distribution above and d3 below, making it the most confusable class---consistent with the d2-vs-d3 pair being the EDD bottleneck (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\nFull per-class recall for all cells appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_class}.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Consistency Across the Grid}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks top-2 by Gini importance in all 9 cells; surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) in 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini; Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals generation depth.\n\nFeature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-2 features (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} + \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) alone reach 91.1\\% of full accuracy (71.5\\%), and the top-5 features achieve 97.6\\% (76.5\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%), with perplexity statistics alone reaching 76.6\\% (vs.\\ surprisal 73.2\\%, other categories ${\\leq}$47\\%).\nVariance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 of 19 features, placing the effective independent dimensionality at ${\\approx}$4--5.\nForward selection independently confirms the dominant pair: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is selected first in 6/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 3/9.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \\textbf{Bold}: top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini).}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.15)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.08), p75\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.14)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), p75\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.07) \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.12)}, m\\_ppl\\,(.11), var\\_ppl\\,(.07), m\\_surp\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, nuc.    & \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.06) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, $\\tau$  & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.25)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.15)}, m\\_surp\\,(.13), var\\_surp\\,(.12), p50\\,(.05) \\\\\nGPT-2\\,XL, top-$k$ & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.24)}, \\textbf{p75\\,(.12)}, var\\_surp\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.04) \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.18)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.15)}, p75\\,(.11), m\\_surp\\,(.07), ttr\\,(.06) \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.21)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.16)}, p75\\,(.12), m\\_surp\\,(.09), ttr\\,(.05) \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & \\textbf{p90\\_ppl\\,(.20)}, \\textbf{var\\_surp\\,(.17)}, p75\\,(.10), m\\_surp\\,(.07), m\\_ppl\\,(.05) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends: perplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease with depth (Spearman $\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians) while repetition features increase, reflecting the complementary narrowing and self-repetition of distribution collapse.\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform: d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 ranges from 0.69 to 0.89---separating adjacent deep generations requires the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC consistently clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold across all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 uniformly falls below $0.70$ ($0.681$--$0.684$).\nThe directional pattern is consistent, but statistical strength is stratified: bootstrap testing ($10{,}000$ resamples, $H_0{:}\\, \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$) yields $p = 0.001$ for Pythia (significant), $p = 0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p = 0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (non-significant).\nAll three models cross the $T{=}0.75$ threshold within the d3--d4 region, establishing a consistent depth-4 decay boundary with graded confidence rather than uniform statistical significance.\nThe d4-vs-d5 saturation is unambiguous across models, supporting the interpretation of d3--d4 as a \\emph{transition zone} from discriminable to indistinguishable regime.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the consistent bottleneck across all 9 cells.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD uses binary-pair classifiers to avoid \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: in Pythia nucleus, d2\\,vs.\\,d3 AUC degrades from 0.804 (4-class) to 0.689 (6-class)---a 14.3\\% relative decline despite identical data---because shared decision boundaries dilute per-pair capacity.\nBinary classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating true adjacent-pair discriminability.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.\nNucleus ($p{=}0.95$) produces the most consistent accuracy (78.2--83.3\\%) and serves as the reference trajectory.\nTemperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.4\\%, OLMo 82.0\\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint---but this acceleration creates a boundary reversal risk: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features, causing cross-strategy classifiers to systematically overpredict depth (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (69.6--76.6\\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens, though feature direction is preserved (cross-model $\\rho = 0.811$).\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp), indicating deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nWe formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\\% four-class accuracy ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ across models; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}), with five features sufficing for 97.6\\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\\leq$0.4pp.\nThe dominant pair (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP) generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.\nRecent work corroborates this connection, identifying entropy decline as a measurable signal of recursive degradation that parallels the tail erosion our features capture~\\cite{shi2025_closer_look}.\nA surface-text ablation reinforces this view: zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2 at 36.2\\%, Qwen2.5-7B at 30.4\\%) both perform near chance despite exhibiting complementary prediction biases, and the 42\\,pp gap confirms that the depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}).\n\nCross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\\% vs.\\ 66.4\\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.\nA multi-temperature ablation ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 1.1]$, Pythia-1.4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}) reinforces this: within-$\\tau$ depth discrimination is robust for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$), but cross-$\\tau$ transfer (mean 46.7\\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%), confirming that strategy-conditioned classifiers are necessary.\nA two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this.\nDifferent strategies produce distinguishable perplexity signatures (top-$k$ yields narrower distributions; temperature sampling produces heavier tails), suggesting that automatic strategy identification is technically feasible, though independent validation on held-out strategy sets is needed.\nAs an alternative, when deployment is restricted to a single platform or API provider, the decoding strategy is typically known a priori, and a strategy-conditioned classifier can be applied directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nDepth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) while filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nA pilot study (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive comparison between RF-filtered and unfiltered conditions ($p>0.4$, $\\mathrm{df}{=}4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation is needed to determine whether depth-based filtering provides a practical advantage over unfiltered training.\nCross-strategy deployment would first require strategy identification to avoid boundary inversion (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods---watermarking~\\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} (requires generator cooperation) and source attribution~\\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} (identifies model, not history)---by characterizing \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator identity~\\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}.\nA layered system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing within controlled generation settings than any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}\nSliding-window depth estimation at the segment level (256--512 tokens) could extend the approach to mixed-depth documents, enabling paragraph-level curation even from partially contaminated content.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nPerplexity tail statistics exhibit inherent resistance to surface-level transformations (paraphrasing, back-translation) that preserve surprisal structure, though robustness to targeted distribution-aware attacks remains unverified.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling behavior.}\nOur preliminary cross-scale observations (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) suggest that depth signal persists at 7--8B scale within controlled generation settings (Llama-3.1-8B: 70.2\\%, Qwen2.5-7B: 60.1\\%), though four confounds---QLoRA quantization, instruction-tuning, smaller sample size, and evaluation limited to nucleus sampling---preclude strong conclusions.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ with monotonic AUC decay, while Qwen2.5-7B achieves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$ via a non-monotonic (U-shaped) trajectory---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ at 1--1.5B.\nPer-step signal strength decreases (mean consecutive AUC ${\\approx}$0.78 vs.\\ ${\\approx}$0.85 at 1--1.5B), consistent with larger models generating more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories (monotonic for Llama-3.1 vs.\\ U-shaped for Qwen2.5-Instruct) suggest that instruction tuning may alter degradation dynamics at high recursion depth, an observation warranting further investigation with controlled base/instruct pairs.\nTail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution rather than model capacity per se, explaining why the signal attenuates but does not vanish; further scaling beyond 8B may require richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) to maintain discriminability.\n",

  "old_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} A systematic $3 \\times 3$ characterization reveals that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} are universal depth markers (top-2 in 9/9 and 8/9 cells), decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories qualitatively, and depth discrimination persists through $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ recursive steps.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization across seven axes (four in main text; three in appendix).} We demonstrate robustness beyond the training grid: cross-model recursive chains (84.75\\%---depth signal accumulates across heterogeneous model boundaries), cross-scale to two 7B architectures (70.2\\% and 60.1\\%, with divergent AUC trajectories), cross-domain transfer to arXiv (74.1\\%), temperature robustness ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$), and reference model independence ($\\Delta{=}4$\\,pp). End-to-end transformers achieve only ${\\sim}$59\\% (19pp below feature-based), confirming that perplexity tail features encode signal inaccessible to standard PLM representations---while requiring only 19 features and a Random Forest for millisecond-scale, GPU-free inference.",
  "new_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} A systematic $3 \\times 3$ empirical characterization reveals that perplexity tail statistics as a feature group dominate depth discrimination (top-2 in 9/9 and 8/9 cells), decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories qualitatively, and depth discrimination persists through $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family (Pythia, $p{=}0.001$; consistent direction across all three families with graded statistical support).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization across seven axes (four in main text; three in appendix).} We demonstrate robustness beyond the training grid: cross-model recursive chains (84.75\\%---preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal), preliminary cross-scale observations on two 7B architectures (70.2\\% and 60.1\\%, with divergent AUC trajectories), cross-domain transfer to arXiv (74.1\\%), temperature robustness ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$), and reference model independence ($\\Delta{=}4$\\,pp). A surface-text ablation (zero-shot LLMs at 30--36\\%, 42\\,pp below RF) confirms that depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone---while requiring only 19 features and a Random Forest for millisecond-scale, GPU-free inference."
}

A surface-text ablation (zero-shot LLMs at 30--36\\%, 42\\,pp below RF) confirms that depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone---while requiring only 19 features and a Random Forest for millisecond-scale, GPU-free inference.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical---it is already occurring in the wild---yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nOur key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{universal and scale-variant} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, perplexity tail statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.\nSurprisingly, in one of two 7B models the AUC trajectory becomes non-monotonic, suggesting that instruction tuning may alter degradation dynamics rather than scale alone driving a qualitative shift.\n\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.\nGiven a text passage, we compute a feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---19 candidates capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity, though high multicollinearity concentrates the signal in ${\\approx}$4--5 effective dimensions (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features})---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).\nTo systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and benchmark.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the EDD metric, and construct a 180K-sample benchmark spanning 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\\times$ 4 depths.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} A systematic $3 \\times 3$ characterization reveals that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} are universal depth markers (top-2 in 9/9 and 8/9 cells), decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories qualitatively, and depth discrimination persists through $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ recursive steps.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization across seven axes (four in main text; three in appendix).} We demonstrate robustness beyond the training grid: cross-model recursive chains (84.75\\%---depth signal accumulates across heterogeneous model boundaries), cross-scale to two 7B architectures (70.2\\% and 60.1\\%, with divergent AUC trajectories), cross-domain transfer to arXiv (74.1\\%), temperature robustness ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$), and reference model independence ($\\Delta{=}4$\\,pp). End-to-end transformers achieve only ${\\sim}$59\\% (19pp below feature-based), confirming that perplexity tail features encode signal inaccessible to standard PLM representations---while requiring only 19 features and a Random Forest for millisecond-scale, GPU-free inference.\n\\end{itemize}",

  "old_string": "These results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence that the depth signal is a property of the recursive process itself rather than of any individual generator.",
  "new_string": "These results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nNevertheless, definitive disentanglement requires a reverse-order chain or architecture-balanced design."
}

mechanism: at low temperature ($\\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \\to 0.42 \\to 0.16$), indicating rapid memorization of the model's own output.\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \\to 1.91 \\to 1.85$)---high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; extraction.}\n\\label{tab:ref_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nReference Model & Architecture & Accuracy (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B (self) & GPT-NeoX & 78.6 $\\pm$ 0.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B (cross) & OLMo & 74.5 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence that the depth signal is a property of the recursive process itself rather than of any individual generator.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results suggest that the depth signal is domain-independent, providing preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a small-scale PoC (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) shows RF-filtered subsets match unfiltered quality with 44\\% of the data; large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",
