```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Additional Results}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}\n\\label{app:topk_trajectories}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.\nConcretely, the d0$\\to$d1 drop in \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\\% (vs.\\ 87\\% for nucleus), and the d2$\\to$d3 reduction is 31\\% (vs.\\ 39\\% for nucleus).\nDespite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Permutation Importance}\n\\label{app:permutation}\n\nTo verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.\nThe top-2 features are stable across measures: \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \\textsc{p75\\_ppl} displaces \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} to rank 3).\nKendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).\nPairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\\rho{=}0.76$.\nPer-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical, confirming that the top features are robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level while per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Class Recall}\n\\label{app:per_class}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).\nDepth~0 recall exceeds 98\\% uniformly.\nAmong generated depths, d2 is the hardest class in every cell; the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class recall (\\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}\n\\label{tab:per_class_recall}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & 99.7 & 81.9 & 58.7 & 72.3 \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & 99.7 & 81.3 & 53.9 & 72.1 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & 99.5 & 72.3 & 45.6 & 57.5 \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & 98.8 & 80.5 & 61.5 & 74.3 \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & 99.7 & 87.7 & 66.1 & 77.2 \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & 99.5 & 78.5 & 51.2 & 65.6 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, nuc.     & 99.2 & 85.9 & \\textbf{73.3} & 77.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$   & 99.5 & 90.9 & 68.8 & 81.9 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.5 & 79.5 & 52.5 & 71.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}\n\\label{app:dcol_iterations}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.\nDeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}\n\\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}\n\nBoth DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.\nThe system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).\nThe user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.\nNo in-context examples are provided.\n\n\\begin{quote}\n\\small\n\\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\\ldots]''\n\n\\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \\{text\\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \\{\\texttt{\"depth\"}: $\\langle$int 0--3$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"confidence\"}: $\\langle$float 0--1$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"reasoning\"}: $\\langle$str$\\rangle$\\}''\n\\end{quote}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Robustness}\n\\label{app:multi_tau}\n\nThe cross-strategy results in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$).\nTo test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\\tau \\in \\{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\\}$ on Pythia-1.4B.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-$\\tau$ robustness.}\nFor $\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\\% to 77.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}), confirming the depth signal is not an artifact of a single temperature.\nThe same top-5 features (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}) dominate across all $\\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.\nTraining loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \\to 0.42 \\to 0.16$), indicating rapid memorization of the model's own output.\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \\to 1.91 \\to 1.85$)---high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes \\emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$\\tau$ non-transferability.}\nLeave-one-$\\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross})---worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nHoldout $\\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176), confirming that different temperatures produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.\nThis extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific, reinforcing the need for strategy-conditioned classifiers.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-$\\tau$ 4-class classification (Pythia-1.4B, 5-fold CV). d$i$v$j$: pairwise AUC for depth $i$ vs.\\ depth $j$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_within}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ & 4-class acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC & d0v1 AUC & d2v3 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 77.3 & 0.842 & 0.996 & 0.764 \\\\\n0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\\\\n0.9 & 75.9 & 0.808 & 0.991 & 0.781 \\\\\n1.1 & 57.2 & 0.528 & 0.988 & 0.599 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-$\\tau$ leave-one-out transfer (Pythia-1.4B). Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) & 95\\% Wilson CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 & [21.3, 23.6] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; $^{**}$\\,: $p<0.01$.}\n\\label{tab:curation_comparisons}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta$ PPL & $t$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nrf\\_strict vs random\\_strict     & 0.429 & $-$7.05 & 0.002$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_moderate vs random\\_moderate & 0.242 & $-$3.77 & 0.020$^*$    \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs random\\_soft         & 0.328 & $-$17.98 & ${<}$0.001$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs unfiltered           & 0.043 &    0.93 & 0.407        \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Reference Model Ablation}\n\\label{app:ref_ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Reference model ablation. Classification accuracy (5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B generated text (nucleus, d0--d3) using different reference models for feature extraction.}\n\\label{tab:ref_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nReference Model & Architecture & Accuracy (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B (self) & GPT-NeoX & 78.6 $\\pm$ 0.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B (cross) & OLMo & 74.5 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nNevertheless, definitive disentanglement requires a reverse-order chain or architecture-balanced design.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a small-scale PoC (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) shows RF-filtered subsets match unfiltered quality with 44\\% of the data; large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark ```text
```
```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract a compact statistical fingerprint---19 candidate features dominated by perplexity tail statistics---computed against a fixed reference model.
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%), with perplexity tail statistics emerging as consistent depth markers across all studied configurations.
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized model family (Pythia, $p{=}0.001$; consistent direction across all three families with graded statistical support; \S4.4).
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% (d1--d3 balanced accuracy: 55.4\%, $1.7\times$ chance); cross-model recursive chains---where each depth is generated by a different architecture---achieve 84.75\% accuracy, providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal.
8	Preliminary cross-scale observations on two 7B-scale generators yield 70.2\% accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$) and 60.1\%, with divergent AUC trajectories possibly linked to instruction-tuning effects.
9	Applied to WikiText-103 and C4 web-crawl text, the classifiers maintain high d0 accuracy on editorial text while revealing model-dependent false positive rates on heterogeneous web content.
10	\end{abstract}
```

```text
0	\section{Generalization and Robustness}
1	\label{sec:generalization}
2	
3	We test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating generalization along seven axes: four in detail below---classifier choice (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and preliminary cross-scale observations (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale})---and three in the Appendix (end-to-end learning, temperature robustness, and cross-boundary transfer).
4	
5	\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}
6	\label{sec:gen:baselines}
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates three classifier families---bagging (RF), gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM)---on the same 19 features across three atlas cells spanning the full accuracy range.
9	Per-cell spread is $\leq$0.4pp, establishing a feature-determined ceiling independent of the learning algorithm.
10	On Pythia nucleus, CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020} (78.1\%) and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} (78.2\%) match the within-cell RF of Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} within 0.1\,pp (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected); all feature-based methods significantly outperform CE-MLP (74.9\%, $p<0.001$) and logistic regression (75.0\%).
11	
12	\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}
13	As a complementary ablation, we test whether depth can be estimated from surface text alone---without the targeted distributional features our pipeline extracts.
14	Single-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%); two zero-shot LLMs---DeepSeek V3.2 (36.2\%, 95\% CI $[32.0, 40.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (30.4\%, $[26.5, 34.6]$; each 500 samples, prompt template in Appendix~\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt})---both fail to distinguish recursive depth, performing near chance (25\%).
15	The 42\,pp gap between RF (78.2\%) and both LLMs confirms that the depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone; this gap exceeds the CI half-width (${\approx}$4\,pp) by an order of magnitude, ensuring robustness at $n{=}500$.
16	
17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\small
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WJpS8teyep5gvLKFKuG5yW`</summary>

```text
160	This divergence suggests the U-shape observed in Qwen is model-specific---possibly related to its instruction-tuning regime (RLHF/DPO)---rather than a scale-dependent phenomenon.
161	The Llama-3.1 result demonstrates that monotonic decay \emph{does} replicate at 7--8B scale for at least one architecture, strengthening the generality of the decay pattern as the default behavior.
162	
163	\paragraph{Caveats.}
164	For Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\%) does not exclude sub-60\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\% range at 1--1.5B.
165	The divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.
166	Both models are instruction-tuned, introducing a confound absent in the base-model experiments: RLHF/DPO alignment may independently shape the feature trajectory in ways not fully attributable to recursive depth alone.
167	Cross-model recursive chains (84.75\%) and cross-domain transfer (arXiv 74.1\%) further confirm signal universality (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_boundary}).
168	
169	\paragraph{Cross-scale confounds.}
170	Four confounds limit these cross-scale results: (1) 4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B), (2) instruction-tuning (RLHF/DPO, absent in base-model experiments), (3) smaller sample size (500 vs 5{,}000), and (4) evaluation limited to nucleus sampling.
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation and showed that a compact feature set dominated by perplexity tail statistics achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ across models; \S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance and classifier-agnostic convergence within $\leq$0.4pp.
5	The dominant feature group---perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9 cells, \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 under SHAP)---generalizes across the three studied model families without retraining, making depth estimation a candidate early-warning signal for model collapse~\cite{shumailov2024_nature} within this experimental scope.
6	Recent work corroborates this connection, identifying entropy decline as a measurable signal of recursive degradation that parallels the tail erosion our features capture~\cite{shi2025_closer_look}.
7	A surface-text ablation reinforces this view: zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2 at 36.2\%, Qwen2.5-7B at 30.4\%) both perform near chance despite exhibiting complementary prediction biases, and the 42\,pp gap confirms that the depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}).
8	
9	Cross-strategy transfer remains the primary bottleneck (59.1\% vs.\ 66.4\% cross-model), with the temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion making single-classifier deployment across unknown decoding strategies infeasible.
10	A multi-temperature ablation ($\tau \in [0.5, 1.1]$, Pythia-1.4B; Appendix~\ref{app:multi_tau}) reinforces this: within-$\tau$ depth discrimination is robust for $\tau \leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$), but cross-$\tau$ transfer (mean 46.7\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\%), confirming that strategy-conditioned classifiers are necessary.
11	A two-stage pipeline---first identifying the decoding strategy, then applying a strategy-conditioned depth classifier---could address this.
12	Different strategies produce distinguishable perplexity signatures (top-$k$ yields narrower distributions; temperature sampling produces heavier tails), suggesting that automatic strategy identification is technically feasible, though independent validation on held-out strategy sets is needed.
13	As an alternative, when deployment is restricted to a single platform or API provider, the decoding strategy is typically known a priori, and a strategy-conditioned classifier can be applied directly.
14	
15	\paragraph{Depth-graduated data curation.}
16	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction~\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
17	Depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) while filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
18	A pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive comparison between RF-filtered and unfiltered conditions ($p>0.4$, $\mathrm{df}{=}4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation is needed to determine whether depth-based filtering provides a practical advantage over unfiltered training.
19	Cross-strategy deployment would first require strategy identification to avoid boundary inversion (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
22	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods---watermarking~\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} (requires generator cooperation) and source attribution~\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} (identifies model, not history)---by characterizing \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator identity~\cite{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms}.
23	A layered system combining watermark presence, source model, and recursive depth could support more nuanced data auditing within controlled generation settings than any single method.
24	
25	\paragraph{Mixed-depth documents.}
26	Sliding-window depth estimation at the segment level (256--512 tokens) could extend the approach to mixed-depth documents, enabling paragraph-level curation even from partially contaminated content.
27	
28	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
29	Perplexity tail statistics exhibit inherent resistance to surface-level transformations (paraphrasing, back-translation) that preserve surprisal structure, though robustness to targeted distribution-aware attacks remains unverified.
30	
31	\paragraph{Scaling behavior.}
32	Our preliminary cross-scale observations (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) suggest that depth signal persists at 7--8B scale within controlled generation settings (Llama-3.1-8B: 70.2\%, Qwen2.5-7B: 60.1\%), though four confounds---QLoRA quantization, instruction-tuning, smaller sample size, and evaluation limited to nucleus sampling---preclude strong conclusions.
33	Llama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ with monotonic AUC decay, while Qwen2.5-7B achieves $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$ via a non-monotonic (U-shaped) trajectory---exceeding the $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ at 1--1.5B.
34	Per-step signal strength decreases (mean consecutive AUC ${\approx}$0.78 vs.\ ${\approx}$0.85 at 1--1.5B), consistent with larger models generating more fluent text with narrower perplexity distributions.
35	The divergent AUC trajectories (monotonic for Llama-3.1 vs.\ U-shaped for Qwen2.5-Instruct) suggest that instruction tuning may alter degradation dynamics at high recursion depth, an observation warranting further investigation with controlled base/instruct pairs.
36	Tail erosion is driven by retraining on one's own output distribution rather than model capacity per se, explaining why the signal attenuates but does not vanish; further scaling beyond 8B may require richer feature sets (e.g., attention entropy or generation diversity metrics) to maintain discriminability.
37	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating generalization along seven axes: four in detail below---classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and preliminary cross-scale observations (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale})---and three in the Appendix (end-to-end learning, temperature robustness, and cross-boundary transfer).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates three classifier families---bagging (RF), gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM)---on the same 19 features across three atlas cells spanning the full accuracy range.\nPer-cell spread is $\\leq$0.4pp, establishing a feature-determined ceiling independent of the learning algorithm.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match the within-cell RF of Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} within 0.1\\,pp (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected); all feature-based methods significantly outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%, $p<0.001$) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nAs a complementary ablation, we test whether depth can be estimated from surface text alone---without the targeted distributional features our pipeline extracts.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); two zero-shot LLMs---DeepSeek V3.2 (36.2\\%, 95\\% CI $[32.0, 40.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (30.4\\%, $[26.5, 34.6]$; each 500 samples, prompt template in Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt})---both fail to distinguish recursive depth, performing near chance (25\\%).\nThe 42\\,pp gap between RF (78.2\\%) and both LLMs confirms that the depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text alone; this gap exceeds the CI half-width (${\\approx}$4\\,pp) by an order of magnitude, ensuring robustness at $n{=}500$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.7pp due to matched-fold evaluation.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence across five structurally different classifiers---spanning bagging, gradient boosting, ordinal regression, and ensemble detection---confirms the signal resides in the features, not the learning algorithm.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline while d2 drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\nExcluding d0, d1--d3 balanced accuracy averages 55.4\\% (chance = 33\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (chance = 33\\%).\nd2 recall varies sharply: nucleus holdouts retain 30--54\\%, top-$k$ holdouts fall to 8--19\\%, and temperature holdouts span 3.7--39.5\\% (median 8.9\\%).\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature inverts the d1/d2 boundary (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5); per-strategy z-score normalization partially recovers it but leaves a residual gap (Limitations).\nA multi-temperature ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}) confirms that within-$\\tau$ depth signal holds for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ (d1v2 AUC $>0.80$) but collapses at $\\tau{=}1.1$; cross-$\\tau$ LOO transfer (mean 46.7\\%) is harder than cross-strategy transfer.\nTemperature variations ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$) do not fundamentally alter depth discriminability within a fixed strategy; classification accuracy remains within 3pp of the $\\tau{=}0.7$ baseline across all tested temperatures. Full multi-$\\tau$ analysis is provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}.\n\nEnd-to-end PLMs (DeBERTa-v3, RoBERTa) achieve only ${\\sim}$59\\%, lagging RF by 19pp, confirming depth signal resides in perplexity tail statistics inaccessible to standard PLM representations (Appendix~\\ref{app:e2e}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Scale Analysis}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossscale}\n\nThe preceding analyses use 1--1.5B parameter models.\nTo test whether depth signal persists in substantially larger generators, we extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\cite{qwen2.5} and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\cite{llama3}, both under 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning, generating depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\ldots, 5\\}$ with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth).\nFeature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and 19-feature pipeline as all prior experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-scale comparison: 4-class RF accuracy (\\%, depths 0--3, nucleus), $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ (depths 0--5), and d0-vs-d1 AUC. CI: 95\\% bootstrap. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & 4-class acc & $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL (1.5B) & 83.3 & 4 & 0.999 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.8 & 4 & 0.997 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 4 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 70.2\\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 60.1\\,[58.0, 62.1] & ${>}5$ & 0.969 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThree findings emerge:\n\n\\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}\nBoth 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\\% (95\\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$), confirming that recursive depth remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\\times$ larger than the reference model.\nDepth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).\n\n\\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}\nAt threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.\nBoth larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Consecutive binary AUC for 7--8B models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$). Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay (d4v5 drops below $T{=}0.70$); Qwen2.5-7B shows a non-monotonic (U-shaped) pattern with all pairs above $T$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 & d3v4 & d4v5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 0.969 & 0.729 & 0.709 & 0.732 & 0.772 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories across architectures.}\nLlama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay (0.949 $\\to$ 0.877 $\\to$ 0.771 $\\to$ 0.716 $\\to$ 0.618), consistent with the pattern observed at 1--1.5B scale.\nIn contrast, Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3: consecutive AUC drops from 0.969 (d0v1) to a minimum of 0.709 (d2v3), then \\emph{rises} to 0.732 (d3v4) and 0.772 (d4v5).\nThis divergence suggests the U-shape observed in Qwen is model-specific---possibly related to its instruction-tuning regime (RLHF/DPO)---rather than a scale-dependent phenomenon.\nThe Llama-3.1 result demonstrates that monotonic decay \\emph{does} replicate at 7--8B scale for at least one architecture, strengthening the generality of the decay pattern as the default behavior.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.}\nFor Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\\%) does not exclude sub-60\\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\\% range at 1--1.5B.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.\nBoth models are instruction-tuned, introducing a confound absent in the base-model experiments: RLHF/DPO alignment may independently shape the feature trajectory in ways not fully attributable to recursive depth alone.\nCross-model recursive chains (84.75\\%) and cross-domain transfer (arXiv 74.1\\%) further confirm signal universality (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_boundary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scale confounds.}\nFour confounds limit these cross-scale results: (1) 4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B), (2) instruction-tuning (RLHF/DPO, absent in base-model experiments), (3) smaller sample size (500 vs 5{,}000), and (4) evaluation limited to nucleus sampling.",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018Ygb8GGVeMeBEuWvg7je1D`</summary>

```text
315	The preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.
316	In practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.
317	We probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.
318	
319	\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}
320	We construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.
321	Each recursive step uses a \emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.
322	A 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\% accuracy (95\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).
323	Pairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.
324	These results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.
325	
326	\paragraph{Confound discussion.}
327	The heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).
328	However, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.
329	The observed 84.75\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.
330	Nevertheless, definitive disentanglement requires a reverse-order chain or architecture-balanced design.
331	
332	\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}
333	All prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.
334	To test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).
335	Five-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\% $\pm$ 2.0\% accuracy, compared to ${\sim}$78\% on the WikiText seed.
336	Per-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.
337	The 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.
338	These results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.
339	
340	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
341	\label{app:extended_discussion}
342	
343	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
344	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
345	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
346	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.
347	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
348	
349	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
350	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
351	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
352	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
353	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
354	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
```

```text
218	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).
219	
220	\begin{table}[h]
221	\centering
222	\small
223	\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}
224	\label{tab:calibration}
225	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrccc@{}}
226	\toprule
227	Model & Source & $n$ & d0 (\%) & FPR (\%) & 95\% Wilson CI \\
228	\midrule
229	Pythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 & --- \\
230	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\
231	\addlinespace
232	GPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 & --- \\
233	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 & [21.3, 23.6] \\
234	\addlinespace
235	OLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 & --- \\
236	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\
237	\bottomrule
238	\end{tabular}
239	\end{table}
240	
241	On WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.
242	On C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\%) vs.\ Pythia (20.2\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\%).
243	C4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.
244	The model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.
245	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
246	
247	\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}
```

```text
Everything looks good. Let me now verify the Wilson CI values I computed are correct:

For Wilson CI: The formula is:
p_hat ± z * sqrt(p_hat * (1 - p_hat) / n + z^2 / (4*n^2)) / (1 + z^2/n)

But the Wilson interval is actually:
Lower, Upper = (p + z^2/(2n) ∓ z * sqrt(p*(1-p)/n + z^2/(4n^2))) / (1 + z^2/n)


Now I'm checking my calculations for z=1.96 at 95% confidence. For Pythia C4 with p=0.202 and n=5000, the normal approximation gives me 0.202 ± 0.011, which yields [0.191, 0.213]. Let me verify the same approach for GPT-2 XL C4 with p=0.224. I'm verifying the confidence intervals are correct—they match what I reported as percentages. Now I need to check the generalization.tex file to make sure there aren't any other references to "Baseline Comparison" in section 4.1, and I should look through the other files to see if anything else needs updating.
```
